Mr. Sculimbrene reads aloud a letter from the first grade team.  Dear members of the Lunenburg School 
Committee, the first grade team is writing to express serious concern about the proposed change from five 
first grade classrooms of approximately 23 students each to four classrooms of 30 students or more each. 
We want to begin by acknowledging and appreciating the difficult decisions you are facing and working 
within what we understand to be an extremely challenging and at times seemingly impossible budget. We 
recognize the care and thought that goes into balancing financial constraints with the needs of students, 
staff, and the broader community, and we are grateful for your continued commitment to our schools. At the 
same time, we feel strongly that this proposed change will have a significant impact on our youngest 
learners. First grade is a foundational year for literacy development. It is during this critical period that our 
students not only learn to read, but build their foundation for future learning and shape their entire academic 
trajectory. Increasing class sizes to 30 students significantly limits a teacher's ability to provide the 
instruction that early literacy demands. In smaller classes, we can more effectively deliver mandatory 
curriculum, identify gaps, and tailor instruction to meet diverse needs. With 30 students, that level of 
attention becomes extremely difficult, if not impossible. As classroom teachers with little to no support 
already, we feel confident that our struggling learners will not receive the individualized instruction and 
intervention they deserve and require. This poses tremendous long-term academic consequences. Research 
has consistently shown that early grade size matters most. Investing in smaller classes in first grade is not 
simply beneficial. It is essential. We are deeply concerned about changing the class size particular 
particularly as the number and intensity of student behavior challenges continues to increase each year. We 
are already supporting students with a wide range of social, emotional, and behavioral needs, often without 
staffing or resources for us to do so effectively. Many of our young students are arriving in our classroom 
needing support in managing big emotions, dealing with trauma, following rules and routines, and 
maintaining focus. Addressing these needs takes substantial time, and often disrupts instruction, reduce 
learn reducing learning opportunities for all students. While larger class sizes may seem like a practical 
response to budget or staffing challenges, they often lead to unintended consequences for teacher morale, 
effectiveness, and retention. As class sizes grow without additional support, we have less capacity to 
provide individualized instruction and are forced to focus on more classroom management than meaningful 
learning. This shift will increase stress, make student needs harder to meet, and will lead to burnout, 
frustration, and a diminished sense of effectiveness in our classrooms. We respectfully urge the committee 
to carefully reconsider this proposal and prioritize maintaining small class sizes in first grade, smaller class 
sizes in first grade. Supporting our youngest learners at this critical stage is one of the most important 
investments we can make in their future. Thank you for your time, your service, and your consideration. 
Sincerely, Christa Palma, Jackie Favreau, Kara Slattery, Pattie Kilcommins, and Mike Courtemanche

1025 Massachusetts Avenue, Lunenburg, Massachusetts 01462 
978 582-4100 ~ fax 978 582-4103 
Mr. Sculimbrene reads a letter from Nicole Bienvenu on behalf of the second grade team.  Dear Mr. 
Sculimbrene, Ms. Brzozoski, Miss Gilman and Ms. Young.  We are sending this to you today as a team of 
professional educators with a collective 71 years of teaching and practical experience at the Lunenburg 
Primary School to express both our unwavering commitment for to what is best for our second grade 
students and also our concern for those students due to potential staffing changes in the coming year. To 
begin, we want to thank you for the time for taking the time to hear our concerns and for your advocacy on 
our schools and students during this challenging fiscal time. We are aware of and understand the budget 
constraints all too well as we have been experiencing this difficult environment for some time. We 
appreciate the difficult situation you face balancing priorities and resources. We would like to emphasize 
and make you aware that any staff cuts resulting in class sizes of 30 or more students at the primary level 
will lessen our ability to educate students of Lunenburg effectively. The result of this student to teacher ratio 
will negatively impact our students, their safety and their academic, social and emotional progress. 
Currently the struggles we are facing in our classroom include substantial and growing number of students 
with social emotional dysregulation major unsafe physical and verbal behaviors and a lack of staff to 
support these ever increasing needs. The current staffing shortage has already made it very challenging to 
adequately meet academic, social, emotional, and behavioral needs of students at the primary school. 
Staffing reductions would only make that situation worse. We believe drawing on our extensive experience 
as educators that at those levels of student to teachers, it would become impossible to give our second grade 
students the focused instruction they require without even meeting the basic requirement for adequate space 
to safely educate our students. We are facing a current reality in education where every year our students 
come to us with more and more needs, specifically social, emotional, and behavioral needs. We need to 
acknowledge this reality and be proactive in supporting our student needs. Reducing staff at the Lunenburg 
Primary School will make an already difficult situation impossible. Our school is staffed by dedicated, 
experienced, and compassionate educators who consistently go above and beyond to ensure our students 
receive the support they need. We all agree our students deserve a robust and effective public education. We 
need and appreciate your support and advocacy in order to continue to deliver what our students need to be 
successful. For all of these reasons, we are asking you not to cut classroom educator positions at the Primary 
School. We have one more critical area of concern. Given the significant challenges we are facing in our 
school year, our assistant principal has been vital in supporting us. He consistently goes above and beyond 
to support staff and students. Our school would be less safe and less functional without a full-time assistant 
principal. Please also consider not cutting this full-time essential position. Our Lunenburg Primary School 
mission statement states, "Our mission, we prepare our students for lifelong learning and responsible 
community membership. Our vision, the Lunenburg Primary School is a child- centered school devoted to 
providing high quality early childhood education for all students. We're a community of teachers, students, 
and parents working together to create a safe and respectable, respectful learning environment”. Our current 
knowledge of child development guides the school's beliefs and practices and provides the foundation for 
student learning. Please help us and advocate for what our students deserve in order to learn at the 
Lunenburg Primary School and help us live up to our mission statement. Respectfully, Nicole Bienvenu, 
Melissa Bourgeois, Britney Gibbons, Julie Hudson, Sara Kenney, Laura Refrigeri, and Karen Hurley.

4. Chairs Report I just want to make four quick points. Number one, this has been an especially unusual 
form of torture. Making people who care a lot about education being legally compelled to disassemble 
the school district they send their children to is terrible. The balance budget that we put forward on 
Wednesday and the revised balance budget that we put forward tonight are mandated by the town 
charter. This is not something that anyone would choose to do or want to do. We met with the booster 
parents for athletics. We're going to meet with the booster parents for other extracurricular activities. 
Our hope is that the only time we talk about this balanced budget is when we are obligated to and that's 
it. It reminds me of the process of watching my grandfather die. My dad's dad, he was named Meyer. He 
was born in Italy and he came here when he was a young man and he had very serious diabetes. And I 
lived in Ohio and they lived in in Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania. And we would visit, every three months. 
And each time I would go, I would see him getting sicker and sicker. I would see, a toe get taken by the 
doctor and then a foot. And it just kept getting worse and worse and worse. And I think that what we 
need to understand is that there is a point of no return for the school district. And we are rapidly 
approaching the point of no return. It is a mathematical certainty at a certain point that this will not 
continue to work because funding from the state government is largely premised on the number of 
students. If we have a reason for some students to leave that would reduce funding and then that would 
in turn cause more students to leave. We need to be very careful as a community to avoid the death 
spiral and we are very close to being at that point. Second thing I want to say is the removal of fire. You 
know, I thought it was a negative. I still think that it was not great, but Anna Lockwood called me on 
Sunday and she gave me a pep talk. I am really grateful for the pep talk. Her pitch was this, a smaller 
override is more likely to pass. And I think that she is right.  Anna is a unique person. I've never met 
anybody like her. I'm very glad that I can call her my friend. And I am very glad that on a Sunday 
afternoon when it should not be snowing, she can call and tell me, "Look at this differently. You may 
have been wrong." And I think she was right. The third thing is, as we sit here and talk about all the 
things that we would have to cut under a balanced budget, I am reminded of what our last great president 
said when people were booing. He said, "Don't boo, vote." So if we're complaining, go vote. If we are 
advocating to the school committee, go vote. If you need somebody to sponsor you to speak at town 
meeting, I will make sure that happens. But anybody who is agitated needs to be motivated. And the 
final thing is we have got to stop the process of doing this year in and year out. We need to engage in 
true long-term planning. That was the way I was sold on the tri-board meeting. Unfortunately, for a 
whole host of reasons, we didn't get to the point where we were engaging in long-term planning because 
the crises in front of us became too big. But we do need to go back to that model. I think the idea of 
bringing back things like vocational programs to the school, even if it means that we'd have to 
disentangle ourselves from Monty Tech that is something that has real long-term value. I think that 
looking at how we utilize school property has real long-term value. I think that one of the things that we 
could try to do is find other places where there is synergy such as if we have an athletic trainer could 
that person also provide some sort of basic callisthenic services at Eagle House. All of these things are 
things that we need to do and we need to do not just from February until May, but we need to do them 
all the time year round and we need to have long-term planning. I look at when we go to Fitchburg for 
===PAGE 9===
 
1025 Massachusetts Avenue, Lunenburg, Massachusetts 01462 
978 582-4100 ~ fax 978 582-4103 
track meets, they took out a huge amount of money and a huge amount of effort and they got the solar 
canopy built for their parking lots. We need to start thinking about things like that. All of those things 
planned out over 10 years will work. And the reason I know that is because when I started my law firm, 
I had a very nice job at a boutique firm and I decided that I was going to do it by myself with one of my 
very good friends and we planned and we saved and we went without a salary for almost a year to make 
it work. If we can if I can do that and I am not a great financial wizard, I know that we as a town can 
make this happen. We need to engage in long-term planning and stop crisis management. It is not a 
crisis if it happens every year. It's just what is expected. So, we've got to do better. 
 
5. Old Business 
Action 
a. FY 27 Balanced Budget Vote Dr. Fortuna shares a slideshow to explain the consequences of the 
proposed balanced budget. Ms. Gilman makes a motion to not pay MASC dues of $6500 for

accept this budget as presented, Mr. Sculimbrene seconds the motion, all approve 
 
6. Public Comment Mary Foyle, 985 Flat Hill Road As someone who's worked in this district almost 25 
years, and who wears a few different hats. I do get feedback from a number of people, sometimes 
unsolicited. I do want to point out, we have been in this position before, I think data probably shows 
statewide that when we went through our first cuts in 2008 that some of us lived through I think we 
didn't get back to 2008 numbers till covid hit because there was covid money in there. So education is 
often first to be cut and one of the last to come back. But I will also say speaking generally just about 
public funds in general, I have students in the school system. I represent teachers across the school 
system. So I feel I can understand the many stakes that are involved in there. But I'm also a person who 
lives in the town and I really have to be cognizant of the fact that police will be looking at cuts in this 
like other places are looking at cuts without an override. So even if some people think, well, the school 
hasn't done this, the school hasn't done that. I know we keep talking about last year's cuts, but I sat in a 
department that took cuts in FY25 as well. So the cuts go beyond just last year and the impacts we're 
feeling go beyond just cuts from last year. And those cuts are only going to continue if we don't pass an 
override. This does somewhat give us a short-term solution. If we don't do an override, and I know this 
was spoken about at the last meeting, and this just doesn't mean for schools, it will cost the town more to 
not do an override than it will cost to do an override. That cost won't just be financial. That cost will be 
the courses students may not take. That cost will be a life that's not saved because an EMT cannot show 
up soon enough. That cost will be a car accident or a robbery or you fell for a scam and the police just 
simply cannot be responding to it. So those costs will hit in a variety of ways. In this town, even, if we 
look at the buildings this notwithstanding we keep making short-term decisions because we don't want 
to spend the money right now. But I think we can come up with a laundry list. I know Mr. Sculimbrene, 
you said one the other night, with the air conditioning in the other building. I think we can come up with 
a laundry list of times we try to save money across the town and you end up paying for it far more down 
the road. So, I would encourage people that are on the fence, come give me your unsolicited comments. 
I will say things back as well, I'll put myself out there, but it will I will guarantee if the override does not 
go through, it will cost the town more. 
 
Chris Sullivan 108 Highland Street I will plead and say show up on May 2nd, show up on May 16th, 
show up in November.  Yes the people we elect have left us out to dry and yet continually year after

In accordance with the requirements of the Open Meeting Law, please be advised that this meeting is being recorded and broadc ast 
live over the Lunenburg Public Access channel, on Facebook Live on the Public Ac cess Facebook page. 
(http://www.facebook.com/Lunenburg-Public-Access-Cable-157427270975305/) and will be uploaded to the Lunenburg Access 
YouTube after the meeting. 
 
The agenda lists all the topics which may be discussed at the meeting and those reasonably anticipated by the Chair.   Votes may be 
taken as a result of these discussions.  Not all items listed may in fact be discussed and other items not listed may be brought up for 
discussion to the extent permitted by Open Meeting Law 
 
1. Call to Order at 7:00 by  Mr. Sculimbrene 
 
2. Pledge of Allegiance  
 
3. Public Comment Eden Perrine 18 White Tail Crossing I am going to advocate about school sports 
counting as gym credits for high school students.  I have multiple reasons behind why this would be 
beneficial for students and for the athletic department.  The first supporting reason this would be 
beneficial is that it would increase the number of people involved in school sports.  If students could free 
their schedule of a gym class and instead play an afterschool sport it would allow students to fulfill other 
credits while still being able to take classes they are interested in taking. An increase in the number of 
kids trying out for sports they would be interested in participating would be instrumental as the teams 
would be bigger.  In the fall of 2025, the boys varsity and jv soccer teams barely had enough students to 
field a game. They resorted to a large number of players swinging for both teams.  With more students 
on the team, they would be able to have two full teams, which would fill the field positions and still 
leave substitutions. There have been multiple studies showcasing that students who participate in after 
school sports have overall better mental health.  A study published in the journal of adolescent health 
found that students who play an afterschool sport had less stress and better mental health as young 
adults.  In a study, they surveyed 850 students from 10 different schools about participation in school 
sports and met up with them again 3 years after graduation and found those that were involved in school 
sports had lower depression symptoms, lower stress, and better mental health than those who did not 
play any sports.  The study co-author Katherine Sabistston even made the statements “team sports offer 
a heightened emphasis on group goals, social support, and sense of connection that provides more 
opportunity for learning adaptive coping strategies that can be essential or long –term mental health” 
She explains that students playing after school sports had a good increase in mental health even as they 
Lunenburg Public Schools 
 
 Dr. Jodi Fortuna, Superintendent of Schools  

===PAGE 2===
 
1025 Massachusetts Avenue, Lunenburg, Massachusetts 01462 
978 582-4100 ~ fax 978 582-4103 
got older.  Another reason supporting the idea of after school sports counting for credit is that more 
people joining sports teams would increase the income as students would pay athletic fees and more 
parents/guardians attending these games would add to the interest fee revenue.  We won’t have to worry 
about students not doing PE either. Those who don’t do any sports would still apply for gym credits and 
those who like it will continue.  I asked 53 people from a mixture of grades, non-student athletes vs 
school athletes to get the students perspective.  I created an anonymous survey and the results showed 
that 100% of students said they supported the idea and would be willing to join an afterschool sport if 
they didn’t already if this were to pass.  Though one was concerned that they enjoyed PE and played an 
after school sport, I explained they were still able to choose gym as a class.  My last point explaining 
why this would be beneficial is that students should be able to take classes they look forward to while 
taking other credit inducing classes.  Kids could take two semesters of other credit gain classes while 
still being able to be in classes that they were interested in.  This is why I think that income-wise, mental 
health wise, participation wise and schedule wise this would be beneficial if the school adopted the idea 
that after school sports could count as PE credits for high school. 
Dave Rogers, 82 Highland Street I have been a resident for 50 years, I am a concerned citizen about a 
whole bunch of different things. I don’t think I have heard anyone say if we do an override we will rid 
ourselves of this problem, I hear just the opposite, it may be a few years, how long? Who knows? So 
before I vote on something I’d really like the whole enchilada, as they say.  What is the best projection 
that we can do so we can have a long range and short range ideas?  I know I don’t have to tell you things 
are pretty tough today.   Number 2 we talk about a lot of things, but not necessarily the building and the 
programs that we need as we go on, its scattered her or there and that plays a big role in costs. I’ve heard 
rumblings about, but no real discussion that centers around bleachers, turf, and the buildings and that all 
equates to money.  The Lions Club started the food pantry at TCP, they take care of thousands of people 
a month, when this idea first came about and I was asked to help out if I could, I said yes, but I didn’t 
think there was a big demand, and I was wrong.  Thanksgiving we gave out two big boxes of food to 
125 plus families, this is a testament to the fact that things are not good right now.  I was in CVS one 
time and there was an elderly lady in front of me talking to the pharmacist and said “well, you really 
really have to take these pills, twice a day” and the lady said, well I ran out to quick, and the pharmacist 
said let me see what I can do, he took her aside and told her there was a special on the prescription, but it 
is only good if you take it as it is prescribed. She said ok I will try.  He said you can take two pills every 
single day for 90 days and we will see what we can do after that.  And she said, oh thank you, I hope I 
will feel better.  Can you imagine talking to your grandmother or your aunt?  It happens and it happens 
around here.  I went into my pocket and made sure that lady would be taken care of.  For those of you 
that don’t know, we have had two tragedies in this town when young people had horrific accidents and 
we pulled together, we build houses, we bought houses, because one of the youngsters was accepted at 
the University of Florida on a doctorate and the other one needed some help, so we get it done.  The 
point is we made it happen.  I have confidence we will continue to do that, but we need to keep our 
heads on straight 
Laura Brzozoski May 2, Saturday is town meeting, you need to come and vote and then Saturday May 
16th is the election.  The override has to pass at both town meeting and the ballot box, you need to sign 
up for both. 
Mandy Gilman I took 3 students, 2 seniors and 1 junior, from Lunenburg High School yesterday to 
testify at the state house in support of more school funding.  It was a great day, Jason, Jade, and Kara 
were thoughtful and interested and I hope they enjoyed it.   I had a great day, but I do want people to 
know although depressing listening to all the schools, it is not just a Lunenburg problem, this is a 
statewide problem.  I am always going to hopeful that Margaret Scarsdale, our state rep is working 
incredibly hard to push though changes to Chapter 70 formula.  I am hoping we will get the override and 
limp through a few years and that the Chapter 70 formula changes.

MEETING MINUTES  
 SCHOOL COMMITTEE MEETING  
Wednesday, April 15, 2026 
        LMHS 7:00 – Hybrid 
 
School Committee    Superintendent  
Anthony Sculimbrene Chair - Present   Dr. Jodi Fortuna 
Laura Brzozoski, Vice Chair - Absent    
Mandy Gilman, Secretary – Present 
Emily Young, Member - Present 
Member,TBD  
     Recording Secretary 
     Courtney Fuller 
Guests:   Christopher McNamara – Business Administrator, Dr. Kimberly Sarfde – Director of Teaching and Learning, Sibyl Kaufman 
- LEAF 
In accordance with the requirements of the Open Meeting Law, please be advised that this meeting is being recorded and broadc ast 
live over the Lunenburg Public Access channel, on Facebook Live on the Public Access Facebook page. 
(http://www.facebook.com/Lunenburg-Public-Access-Cable-157427270975305/) and will be uploaded to the Lunenburg Access 
YouTube after the meeting. 
 
The agenda lists all the topics which may be discussed at the meeting and those reasonably anticipated by the Chair.   Votes may be 
taken as a result of these discussions.  Not all items listed may in fact be discussed and other items not listed may be brought up for 
discussion to the extent permitted by Open Meeting Law 
 
1. Call to Order at 7:05 by Dr. Fortuna 
 
2. Pledge of Allegiance  
 
3. Public Comment Mandy Gilman, I thank both the chair and the superintendents for their comments, we 
have worked collaboratively for a long time over the last eight months with the rest of the town. There 
has been a lot of discussion about why there is not an option for level services funding, I feel strongly 
that the town, when they didn’t endorse the override last year, asked the group of leaders in the town to 
get together and think about a long term plan.  A long term plan is difficult in municipal finance, but the 
reason our budgets that are being represented include a few different things is because the goal of the tri 
board process was to think two to three years in advance, and I think that some of that is getting lost in 
the conversations. 
 
4. Chairs Report – I have watched as the debate online and in other places has unfolded and I want to 
make two comments.  Everybody in town who did not watch the tri board meeting process, which I 
don’t blame you if you didn’t, has to realize that process gave us so much different access to the town’s 
budgeting process then we have ever had before. This process and how it unfolded over the last eight 
months really did engage in a collaborative process to build budgets.  The town charter makes clear that 
we are responsible for the school district’s budget and the town and select board are responsible for their 
budget.  While the select board has an oversight role over the entire budget, they really are separate and 
distinct.  This year, it felt like everybody was making sure everyone else knew where they were and 
what was going on.  We have never seen so much detail about each town department.  The second thing 
is while everyone has opinions, and everyone has the right to their opinions, at the end of the day we are 
Lunenburg Public Schools 
 
 Dr. Jodi Fortuna, Superintendent of Schools  

===PAGE 2===
 
1025 Massachusetts Avenue, Lunenburg, Massachusetts 01462 
978 582-4100 ~ fax 978 582-4103 
all still neighbors.  If someone, who is your neighbor disagrees with you, or you see fights on Facebook, 
remember that we are all neighbors.  If we are all fighting, it is a beautiful spring in New England, close 
your laptops, push your keyboards away and go for a walk. 
 
5. Student Representative Report – The Primary School has their first grade field trip on May 26. Turkey 
Hill has their third grade field trip on May 27, fourth grade trip is May 29 and the fifth grade is June 3rd.  
The Middle school has their play tomorrow, called Are you Scared Yet.  There is a family DC meeting 
in the auditorium right now.  The students going to Greece leave on Friday.  If you visit our website 
there is a updated information for the seniors. Next week is school vacation.  April 30th is a PTO 
fundraiser at Launch.  Bus sign ups close on May1st, May 2nd is Town meeting, May 16th is Town 
election, May 25th there is no school in observance of Memorial Day and June 18th is the last day of 
school. 
 
6. Review and Approve Minutes 
a. Regular session 3/4/26 Ms. Gilman makes a motion to approve the minutes, Ms. Young 
seconds the motion all approve. 
,  
7. Review and Approve Line Items, Transfers, Warrants 
a. Line Item Transfers  None 
b. Warrants $18,547.98 
c. Donations – Visual Arts student scholarship, this is 2 one time scholarships for a graduating 
senior donated by the artisans of Hickory Hills care of Donna Silverstein Ms. Gilman makes a 
motion to accept the donation, Ms. Young seconds the motion all approve 
 
8. Superintendents Report Dr. Fortuna addresses the upcoming town budget and Proposition 2 ½ 
override votes.  The dedication this community shows to our students and schools is wonderful.  There 
is a strong emphasis on “one town” we all work together.  April 28th at 7:00 at the Eagle House, I invite 
anyone with questions to come meet with our town representatives. 
 
9. New Business 
Action 
a. Leaf Grant Funds Sybil Kaufman, president of LEAF, gives an update for the status of LEAF, to 
date $8,366 has been raised.  The grant cycle will start May 1, 2026 
b. Prism II & III Grant and Fresh Grant Proposals 2026 Dr. Sarfde presents two grants, one is a 
farm to table grant, and has worked with our Food Service Director to determine how this would 
be used and implemented.  Ms. Young makes a motion to accept the proposal, Ms. Gilman 
seconds the motion all approve.   Dr. Sarfde explains the Prism II and Prism III grants. Ms. 
Gilman makes a motion to approve the submission of the Prism III grant proposal to DESE 
in the amount of $72,250, Ms. Young seconds the motion, all approve.  The Prism III grant is 
for curriculum materials for high quality instruction and potential some professional development, 
focusing on pre-k to grade 5 Ms. Gilman makes a motion to approve the submission of Prism 
III grant, Ms. Young seconds the motion all approve 
c. Scholarship Tax EIN’s the committee has been provided the legal opinion in their folders tonight 
with their assertions.  We are currently determining whose tax id numbers all the scholarships are 
using. 
d. Unified Sports and Co -ops Dr. Fortuna shares information with the committe e regarding 
available unified sports and options. Ms. Hanscom shares her perspective on our district culture 
and availability of activities to any and all students 
e. Budget Quarterly Report Mr. McNamara shares the recent quarterly report

negotiations and Ms. Brzozoski and Ms. Gilman will do the custodial negotiations  
h. Superintendent Evaluation  Dr. Fortuna has added her list of goals and the different artifacts 
linked to her goals into the committees folder to utilize and prepare the evaluation 
i. First Read Policies 3303 Handling of Cash  Receipts, 5503 Student Data Privacy and 
Security, 5701.01 Administration of Medication, 1303.01 Advertising in Schools

MEETING MINUTES  
 SCHOOL COMMITTEE MEETING  
Wednesday, May 20, 2026 
        LMHS 7:00 – Hybrid 
 
School Committee    Superintendent  
Anthony Sculimbrene Chair - Present   Dr. Jodi Fortuna 
Laura Brzozoski, Vice Chair - Present    
Mandy Gilman, Secretary – Present 
Emily Young, Member - Present 
Member,TBD  
     Recording Secretary 
     Courtney Fuller 
Guests:   Christopher McNamara – Business Administrator 
In accordance with the requirements of the Open Meeting Law, please be advised that this meeting is being recorded and broadcast  
live over the Lunenburg Public Access channel, on Facebook Live on the Public Access Facebook page. 
(http://www.facebook.com/Lunenburg-Public-Access-Cable-157427270975305/) and will be uploaded to the Lunenburg Access 
YouTube after the meeting. 
 
The agenda lists all the topics which may be discussed at the meeting and those reasonably anticipated by the Chair.  Votes may be 
taken as a result of these discussions.  Not all items listed may in fact be discussed and other items not listed may be brought up for 
discussion to the extent permitted by Open Meeting Law 
 
1. Call to Order at 7:00 by  Mr. Sculimbrene 
 
2. Pledge of Allegiance  
 
3. Public Comment Jane Hall I’ve been a resident of Lunenburg for 47 years. Not quite an old-timer yet, 
only in age. But I just I want to say to all of you how much I appreciate all of the work that you have 
done on the override, the school committee, our administration, all of you, the teachers, everybody. I'm 
sorry that it failed. Truly am, one of our town members at the town meeting brought up a little bit of 
history from overrides in years gone by. And my children went through those years. So, they're in their 
late 30s, middle to late 40s, and they all suffered through overrides because their mother was usually 
either the chair or the co-chair of the override committees. Some we won and some we lost as you have 
experienced. But what I wanted to just share, if you will, is that we will get through this. As you know, 
we will. The losses are deep. There's no question. But I've heard that there'll be a call for volunteers, for 
people to come in and help in the lower grades with reading. And I think that's wonderful. The more 
public awareness we can bring to all age groups, the better, it is not the solution. Believe me, I don't 
think that's the solution to the problem. But I think having support in the schools from community 
members who, you know, with some training would be really wonderful. It can help in many ways just 
even with helping the students, but also to see what goes on in the school. So, I applaud that and I will 
be one of them for sure, I just I can't say enough. We will get through this. Our kids will be okay and 
they will all go on and be successful upper grade level students, if you will, and colleges. And I look at 
my children and as hard as those struggles were, and they were difficult, some years really difficult, they 
all did okay. So again, thank you for all of the work that you've done and that you will continue to do. 
Thank you. Thank you, Dr. Fortuna, for all of your work and your beautiful letter that went out after. I 
don't think I've ever seen that before. 
Lunenburg Public Schools 
 
 Dr. Jodi Fortuna, Superintendent of Schools  

===PAGE 2===
 
1025 Massachusetts Avenue, Lunenburg, Massachusetts 01462 
978 582-4100 ~ fax 978 582-4103 
Chris Sullivan 108 Highland Ave I want to thank you for all of your efforts, regardless of opinion there 
is a lot of work that went into the entire budget season and I am grateful for the effort and extra time. I 
also wanted to talk about the fifth grade bus release, I was that family that was doing this seven years 
ago with two other moms, we collected data, and we ran surveys we did not prevail at the time.  At the 
time my son was in the fourth grade.  Oddly enough I could have signed my fourth grader out as a 
walker and he could have walked home, but he couldn’t get off the bus by himself, there was a lot of 
interesting logic there.  I am hoping that common sense prevails tonight at you take on that article again. 
 
4. Chairs Report – Mr. Sculimbrene reports the outcome of the override vote was disappointing, but not 
unexpected.  We knew and stated on public record as early as September 2025 that the override effort 
was always going to be a true battle.  The combination of the surplus and the general difficulty of selling 
an override means that this time was from the very beginning a hard sell.  As the chair of this school 
committee I am committed to making sure an override passes as soon as possible.  The next time we 
have the advantage of the lessons from this failure.  In particular, we will make sure that the financial 
house is in order and has been for a substantial period of time.  We are well on our way to meeting this 
mark.  Second, we will only ask for what we actually need, which is what we did this around in the tier 
one budget.  Third we will ask for an override on our own, to make sure we can better control the 
message. Fourth we will have a clearer set of options for voters. We know what is at stake and we will 
continue to fight for the future of the children of Lunenburg.  The result of the vote was a delay not a 
defeat.  America fought for 8 years four months and 15 days to obtain its independence from Great 
Britain, we owe it to our children to fight for at least one more year.  I also want to give a commendation 
to one person. Everybody here has worked really hard this year. Every teacher here, every member of 
the staff, all of the administration, they have worked incredibly hard. And so accommodation for one 
person seems somewhat unfair, but in this case, I think it is deserved. So I wanted to point out the work 
that Yanina Burns has done. And I want to say this year, Miss Burns didn't do just the basics, showing 
up, spending money as authorized, making sure that logistics associated with the job were taken care of 
daily. She made huge strides in keeping kids on the field, and teams competitive. She was at literally 
every sports event I attended this year, whether it was a football game in the fall, a random basketball 
game in the dead of winter, or spring track. She was working, helping, and leading, not with a bullhorn 
or quote unquote supervision, but with roll up her sleeves hustle. I told her when she came before the 
school committee about the budget this year, and it remains true today that Miss Burn's hallmark is 
excellence. Everything she did this year, she did with grace, insight, and skill. Her decision to remain on 
as the AD is huge for both the kids of the town, the kids of Lunenburg, and the town itself. It is critical 
as we approach the severe austerity measures imposed because of the failure of the override that we 
make sure to recognize and deploy Miss Burns. The future is uncertain, but we should always bet on 
people like her. For all of these reasons, I want to highlight Miss Burns as the person that aided the 
district most in 2025 2026 school year from the perspective of the chair of school committee. The last 
thing I want to say is this. I'm not going to be chair anymore. It is too much work. It is very difficult. I 
also think that it's important that we have new ideas and so when she gets here my plan is to nominate 
Laura to be the chair of school committee. I think she will do an excellent job. She has different 
strengths than I do and I think that she will do things her way and that way will go very well. One thing 
that I thing we all need to work on is the length of the meetings, we need to make them shorter and more 
to the point. 
 
5. Student Representative Report – District wide there is no school on May 25th, June 6th there is a bake 
and book sale at the library.  The primary school has their field day on June 11th, Turkey Hill has their 
field day on June 9th.  The middle school has a jazz concert next Wednesday, a concert band and chorus 
concert next Thursday, eighth grade graduation is June 18th.  The high school has graduation on June 6th, 
this Friday is the honors and AP art thesis show

Action 
a. Committee Reorganization 
i. Election of Officers & Appointment of Sub-Committee Members Dr. Fortuna accepts 
nominations for the Chair position of the School Committee. Mr. Sculimbrene nominates 
Ms. Brzozoski as chair, Ms. Young seconds the nomination, Ms. Brzozoski accepts -  Ms. 
Brzozoski is the new Chair  Mr. Sculimbrene is nominated as Vice Chair, Ms. Young 
seconds, all approve, Mr. Sculimbrene accepts. – Mr. Sculimbrene is the new Vice Chair. 
Ms. Brzozoski is provisionally nominated as secretary, Ms. Young seconds all approve.  
Ms. Gilman will be asked at the next meeting if she would like to accept. The committee 
goes through subcommittee positions and decide on which member will be appointed. Mr. 
Sculimbrene makes a motion to accept the reorganization of committee 
responsibilities as proposed, Ms. Young seconds the motion all approve. 
ii. Authorize Policies & Procedures to Remain in Force  Ms. Young makes a motion to 
authorize policies and procedures to remain in force for the 26/27 school year, Ms. 
Brzozoski seconds the motion, all approve 
iii. Annual Student Activity Account Authorization  Ms. Brzozoski makes a motion for 
the annual activity account authorization of $2000 for the Primary School, $2000 for 
Turkey Hill, $5000 for the middle school and $10,000 for the high school, Ms. Young 
seconds the motion all approve 
iv. Annual Town Meeting – Auditorium Waiver Ms. Brzozoski makes a motion to allow 
drinks with lids and food at the annua l town meeting in the LMHS auditorium, Ms. 
Young seconds the motion all approve. 
 
b. Next Steps after Override Dr. Fortuna shares some of our options, we could call a special town 
meeting and election for a school only override, the town manager would not stand in the way if 
we choose to go this way.  We could call for a special town meeting election for a smaller level 
services override, or we could stand firm on the balanced budget.  My recommendation to the 
committee is that we implement the balanced budget and launch a community task force given 
that the override was defeated resoundingly that the margins were not close in vote.  Attempting 
option one or two right now carried extreme political risk.  Returning to the voters immediately 
may be perceived as tone-deaf or dismissive of their decision.  A second defeat would severely 
fracture our community and damage the districts credibility for years to come.  I am 
recommending that the school committee charter for future Lunenburg Schools community task 
force be focused on only the schools, and should include parents, educators, town officials, and 
vocal opponents of the recent override.

MEETING MINUTES  
 SCHOOL COMMITTEE MEETING - RETIREE RECEPTION 
Wednesday, June 3, 2026 
        LMHS 6:30 – Hybrid 
 
School Committee    Superintendent  
Laura Brzozoski, Chair – Present   Dr. Jodi Fortuna 
Anthony Sculimbrene, Vice Chair, Absent    
Mandy Gilman, Secretary – Absent 
Emily Young, Member - Present 
Jean Russell, Member, - Present 
     Recording Secretary 
     Courtney Fuller 
Guests:   Christopher McNamara – Business Administrator, Carla Squier – Primary School Principal, Tim Santry, LMHS Principal, 
Norman Yvon – THES Principal, Eden Perrine – LHS Student 
In accordance with the requirements of the Open Meeting Law, please be advised that this meeting is being recorded and broadcast 
live over the Lunenburg Public Access channel, on Facebook Live on the Public Access Facebook page. 
(http://www.facebook.com/Lunenburg-Public-Access-Cable-157427270975305/) and will be uploaded to the Lunenburg Access 
YouTube after the meeting. 
 
The agenda lists all the topics which may be discussed at the meeting and those reasonably anticipated by the Chair.   Votes may be 
taken as a result of these discussions.  Not all items listed may in fact be discussed and other items not listed may be brought up for 
discussion to the extent permitted by Open Meeting Law 
 
1. Call to Order at 7:00 by Ms. Brzozoski 
 
2. Pledge of Allegiance  
 
3. Public Comment Chris Sullivan, 108 Highland Street, There was a comment made at last meeting 
about adding it to the agenda and there being a discussion, I am very disappointed that it is not on this 
agenda tonight.  I think there is a concerted effort in this town to continue to see those flourish and see if 
there are means and mechanisms to fund the things we are losing.  So just to comment I am disappointed 
it is not on the agenda. 
Laura Barooshian, 389 Mulpus Road We are not borrowing $47 million for the water treatment plant, 
in case you haven’t heard, that just failed.  I made a comment that we are trying to find $10 grand to put 
the music program back in the program, in full transparency, I did speak with Dr. Fortuna and I spoke 
with Kara last week at the band concert.  I would like to get an accounting of the music, specific chorus, 
across the district of the programs, the things that were cut.  I would also as that Mr. Sheldon and Ms. 
McGregor and Mr. Ballister comment on what the priorities are, and as president of the Musicators, 
what can we do to fill in those gaps.  We have such a strong program, last week there were 120 kids on 
the stage and tomorrow those 120 kids will be going for Trills and Thrills, 3 busses full of kids.  I have 
my daughter at home sorting snacks and we got water, so we have some traction.  We are so 
disappointed obviously that the override did not pass and the cuts we are sustaining.  I am making a 
formal request, for the accounting of everything that was reduced and then working with the teachers 
and working with you as well.  What can we fundraise for based on what their priorities are. 
Peter Beardmore 292 Pleasant Street. I want to talk about money, I recognize that the defeat of the 
override was a lot of things, demoralizing is probably very high on that list.  I think it’s caused a lot of 
Lunenburg Public Schools 
 
 Dr. Jodi Fortuna, Superintendent of Schools  

===PAGE 2===
 
1025 Massachusetts Avenue, Lunenburg, Massachusetts 01462 
978 582-4100 ~ fax 978 582-4103 
people to think about what do we do next?  How do we approach this? Two to one majority against 
seems insurmountable obstacle, to think about how we fund the town and our schools over the course of 
the next several years.  I would encourage people to keep an open mind.  I think Selectmen Emkey, said 
very astutely last night that clearly there isn’t an appetite to go back and try to run the same play again 
for an override, given what has happened.  I look at between 700 and 800 votes on the “for side” and I 
think well its five or take what we got last time.  There was an opposition, there was a campaign, and 
there wasn’t a sufficient story ask message campaign on the other side to go and get this thing.  We have 
over 1500 kids in our schools and we came up with 700 to 800 votes, I don’t think we need to bring 
back the same proposal, in fact, I wouldn’t.  I think there are things we can do differently, but we cannot 
give up, we cannot concede.  That budget that we are going to run next year is the level of service that 
we can continue to offer in perpetuity in Lunenburg.  Your job is to represent the kids and that is going 
to seem a little ironic to some people who are here, who were part of the many arguments that I had with 
Carol Archambault over the years about what your job is.  They don’t have a voice, they don’t have a 
vote, and it is really incumbent upon you to make sure that the voice is heard to our executive leaders 
and to our finance committee, to our town manager and to the voters at town meeting and to voters at the 
ballot box.  Candidly there needs to be more of both.  I know there has been talk about do we reopen the 
budget and I think that there is a developing consensus amongst members of your board, members of the 
select board and members of the finance committee to not do that.  I wonder why.  In my opinion, if 
things are as bad as we said they were going to be, and we said they were going to be, and here we are, 
then we need to be clawing and scratching for every dollar we can get.  There is a Senate budget when 
you look at all of the sources and local aid, there is somewhere between $350 and $450,000 depending 
on how you calculate it.  I haven’t looked at the budget itself, but that is what I am told, that is what 
Lunenburg would get if that budget passed.  The problem with municipal finance, of course, or state 
finance is that budget is very unlikely to get through conference committee passed and signed by the 
governor inside of the end of July right?  That’s probably the time frame, the second half of July.  Last 
year was an anomaly, they actually passed it at the end of June, so it could happen, but is unlikely.  So 
what do we do? Do we wait for an official budget and then figure out what to do with this windfall 
money or do we start making a plan now and do we lay the foundation in place to act upon those 
available funds.  My opinion is the latter.  You should be talking about what you would do the X amount 
of money.  You should be making a request to the select board to schedule a special town meeting, 
ideally in the month of August.  There should be an article that gives you some leeway depending upon 
the amount of money that is actually made available.  There is a scenario where the state could pass the 
budget in July and in August we can appropriate several hundreds of thousands of dollars to the school 
department I recognize the fact that plans have to be made, the schedule is set for the high school, layoff 
notices have gone out, teachers have quit, families have decided to leave, all of this is happening right 
now, but I think there are impacts that we could affect across the board that we could put 3 to $400,000 
to good use if we put our minds to it.  I would encourage you to think about that. Nobody is going to do 
it based on what I am hearing at the select board of the finance committee, unless you ask.  I am asking 
you to ask and get organized to come up with a plan.  I know the town manager would certainly be, I am 
speculating, but I am sure she would be ready, willing, and able to help you work through the logistics 
of what that could look like. 
 
4. Chairs Report – Our community lost someone special this week, Haddie, you may have seen 
represented in the myriad of “Hope for Haddie” signs and a lot of the color green, passed away.  She 
was an important member of this community and I would like for us to hold in our hearts her family, 
parents, sisters, friends, those who loved her, those who cared about her, our students and our staff, and 
everyone who was affected by the loss of such a bright angel.  So if we could have a moment of silence.  
Everyone knows there is a half day on Friday, so that our staff can attend the funeral.  In the last two 
weeks Massachusetts School Alliance featured some videos about the school committee, we have had an 
===PAGE 3===
 
1025 Massachusetts Avenue, Lunenburg, Massachusetts 01462 
978 582-4100 ~ fax 978 582-4103 
influx of interest in the school.  Members have received threats, peoples said some truly vile things, and 
members were harassed and have had to deal with truly awful, heavily misogynist, hateful things being 
directed to them and harassment that directly came their way.  I am so sorry this happened.  This should 
not be the price to pay for volunteering for school committee.  We have a lack of people in town willing 
to step up and volunteer.  After this past two weeks, I worry we are going to have even less people 
willing,  Tony was originally appointed to fill a vacancy, every one of us up ran unopposed.  It should 
not be this difficult to find people, but it is, and I don’t think that is good for democracy.  I think a lack 
of options is terrible for democracy. The concept of our government hangs on choice and the ability to 
choose our representatives.  Creating a culture where we don’t get to have choices is bad for democracy. 
I want us to be able to debate and discuss options and examine other points of view and share our points 
of view, I want alternate options.  I want people sharing their thoughts.  That is the only way it is going 
to work, so that we can compromise and work on building systems that benefit everyone.  We are a town 
of 11,000 people, we are a small community, we are neighbors, we are friends, we drive on the same 
roads, our kids might go to school together, we see each other out and about.  We call the same town 
home, we are a community. Humans are supposed to care about each other, humans evolve to have 
social groups and social bonds, we evolve to take care of each other, and I am really disappointed right 
now in this community and the wider Massachusetts community, I am very disappointed.  Our job as the 
school committee is to advocate for the schools, to speak for the kids, to make sure that we have an 
adequately funded school system that is going to give the children of Lunenburg an education that will 
help them achieve their goals and become happy, healthy, functioning adults.  That is our responsibility 
that is what we are elected to do.  That is our duty and it is one we all take very seriously.  Our duty is to 
kids, they cannot vote, they can’t even make a public comment at town meeting without a sponsor.  
They have no voice in the decisions that have been made in this town, but the decisions that were made 
are going to affect these kids for the rest of their lives.  With the class sizes next year, there is about 100 
kids or more in each grade, they are going to have class sizes of 27 to 30.  That is over 400 people who 
in this town are very much affected by that.  Asking for the override was our duty as a school committee 
because that is how it was going to get the kids what they need.  There is going to be an election for 
select board, there are two openings and there is going to be a special elections, we need people to run 
for select board, which is on the heels of scrutiny, it is not looking very appealing to run for public office 
right now, but we need people to step up and run.  If you are considering that please do, there will be a 
special election soon, you need to gather signatures to get on the ballot, please consider stepping up and 
serving.

given her the opportunity to take a different academic program, the first part of her senior year she had 
gym, which my understanding is probably not an enjoyable experience as a senior with the freshmen.  I 
would encourage you to consider that.  I would say to look at the encouragement of more participation 
in high school sports, if it’s a sport that may have cuts, like basketball or soccer, there is an opportunity 
for cross country or track.  This fall we had 48 seniors participating in sports, that’s 41% of the senior 
class participated in sports, 8 seniors on the boys basketball team, 4 seniors on the girls basketball team, 
18 seniors in spring and indoor track and 12 boys on the lacrosse team, 5 seniors on the baseball team 
and 4 girls on our softball team.  The more you can participate, you can encourage kids to participate in 
sports, I think a waiver would possibly do that in some of these sports. Mr. Santry talked about the

be in school.  I am now the chair of the parks commission, and I want to start in some capacity a better 
===PAGE 5===
 
1025 Massachusetts Avenue, Lunenburg, Massachusetts 01462 
978 582-4100 ~ fax 978 582-4103 
relationship with the school committee and superintendent’s office regarding scheduling and use of 
fields and so forth.  We need to work collaboratively in a timely manner to get facilities scheduled so we

or anything we need to figure out, we can meet together and sit down and talk.  We need to make sure

Jared Perrine, 18 Whitetail Crossing I want to reiterate in terms of summer programming for the kids, 
we tend to book our summer camps in April for our family, in fact our summer is booked up now, so 
even if the town did offer something, we are full, this happens every year.  As a town we really should 
have those things in place for people to schedule during April break.  Regarding the waiver for PE 
credits, there are many communities in the state that offer this, they don’t call them waivers they call 
them credits.  They offer PE credits for sport, if I can help in any way let me know.  I have been on 
parent advisory councils for 10 years, I am on the middle school council now, I just follow my kids up, 
and if you need help rounding up policies or anything I can do to help.  Ash/West and Littleton are two 
local districts that offer this.  This is in no way discriminatory, the policy would be giving credit to the 
sports that we offer to the kids, I don’t think title IX is a good reason to not offer PE credit for kids that 
want to take other classes.  We offer other opportunities to our kids or at least in my district where I 
work offer early college and all these AP classes and I think any anything that we can do to keep kids in 
school, to keep kids active.  We cut middle school sports, we have to drive kids to every game. You 
know, most people don't get out of work until five o'clock. I don't know how that's going to work for 
families and neither do you. I understand we're all on the same page here, but, anything that we can do 
to get kids involved with sports and to get kids coming to school, kids don't come to school to go to 
history class. Kids come to school to see their friends, to play sports so, however I can help, I'd be happy 
to do that.

In accordance with the requirements of the Open Meeting Law, please be advised that this meeting is being rec orded and broadcast 
live over the Lunenburg Public Access channel, on Facebook Live on the Public Access Facebook page. 
(http://www.facebook.com/Lunenburg-Public-Access-Cable-157427270975305/) and will be uploaded to the Lunenburg Access 
YouTube after the meeting. 
 
The agenda lists all the topics which may be discussed at the meeting and those reasonably anticipated by the Chair.   Votes may be 
taken as a result of these discussions.  Not all items listed may in fact be discussed and other items not listed may be brought up for 
discussion to the extent permitted by Open Meeting Law 
 
1. Call to Order at 7:11 by  Ms. Brzozoski 
 
2. Pledge of Allegiance  
 
3. Public Comment Daryl Plant, 490 Lancaster Ave.  the failure of the override was not primarily the 
result of a tactical error or a messaging problem as suggested by the school committee chair, rather, it 
reflects a deeper issue.  Many Lunenburg residents have lost confidence in our public institutions, 
particularly our schools.  Over the past 7 to 8 years, school leaders have increasingly emphasize social 
equity initiatives, while academic performance has declined.  Regardless of one’s views of these 
initiatives, the results are concerning.  According to statements made by both the superintendent and 
school committee chair at town meeting, the district is close to losing its accreditation and 
approximately 40 to 45% of students are not reading at grade level proficiency.  Many explanations have 
been offered including curriculum changes, funding challenges, and demographic shifts.  However, 
neighboring districts such as Lancaster, Ayer/Shirley, Groton, Pepperell, Townsend, Leominster, and 
Fitchburg have faced similar challenges while achieving stronger outcomes.  According to DESE data, 
parents increasingly feel that the district has focused its attention on struggling students while 
insufficiently challenging those performing at or above grade level.  Every student deserves to be 
appropriately challenged and supported.  Equal opportunity should not require identical outcomes, nor 
should academic excellence be sacrificed in pursuit of other objectives.  At the town meeting, I asked 
the superintendent to provide three specific strategies that would improve student performance, there 
was no response.  If the district hopes to rebuild public trust, it must articulate a clear plan for academic 
improvement and demonstrate measurable results.  To restore confidence and improve out comes, I 
recommend the following:  (1) re-evaluate student support programs, conduct a comprehensive review 
Lunenburg Public Schools 
 
 Dr. Jodi Fortuna, Superintendent of Schools  

===PAGE 2===
 
1025 Massachusetts Avenue, Lunenburg, Massachusetts 01462 
978 582-4100 ~ fax 978 582-4103 
of all nonacademic support programs and staffing.  The goals should be to determine which programs 
demonstrably improve student outcomes and which can be streamlined, restructured, or eliminated.  
Resources should be directed toward services with measurable benefits.  Students with significant 
behavioral or mental health concerns should receive appropriate support in partnership with parents, 
guardians, and qualified professionals (2) Prioritize academic achievement.  Implement an aggressive 
reading, writing, and mathematics remediation program for students who are below proficiency.  At the 
same time, expand opportunities for advanced learners through accelerated coursework, enrichment 
programs, and differentiated instruction.  Students who have mastered grade level materials should be 
encouraged to advance rather than being held back by the pace of the class.  Physical education and 
wellness opportunities should also be expanded recognizing the important connection between physical 
and mental well-being. (3) Review special-ed services.  According to the superintendent, approximately 
25% of students currently receive services through an IEP.  The district should conduct an internal audit 
of all IEP’s to ensure they are appropriate, compliant, and effective.  Students with documented 
disabilities should receive the support they need to succeed while ensuring the resources are allocated 
efficiently and responsibly. (4) Improve efficiency and accountability. By focusing resources on 
academic achievement and evaluating administrative and support costs, the district may identify 
opportunities for savings and improve effectiveness.  Any savings should be used to strengthen 
classroom instruction, preserve extracurricular programs, and reduce financial burdens on taxpayers 
whenever possible.  Most importantly, restoring academic excellence will require accountability at every 
level of leadership.  The community deserves clear goals, transparent reporting, and measurable 
progress.  If current leadership is unable to deliver meaningful improvement, residents should consider 
whether new leadership is necessary to move the district forward. 
Matt Nazarenko 79 Robbs Hill Road Thank you to Dr. Fortuna and Laura for having a meeting a 
couple weeks ago with boosters, that was very helpful.  One of the things out of that was the parents and 
boosters are looking for a by sport line funding.  We were able to find the fiscal year 24 actuals online if 
you go through your budget files and I did put those in the third and fourth column.   I applied 6 1/2% 
compounded annual growth rate to the costs in fy 24 moving through the past year, the second page, the 
last two columns are the impact per athlete.  The black numbers would be revenue, positive meaning 
$350 athletic fee that we would charge the student covers the sport, if you roll that out to the rightmost 
column that would be the revenue impact per sport.  You can see that football is a $20,000 negative 
revenue impact and other sports are positive.  One thing that is concerning to me is that in the meeting 
we had a couple weeks ago, it was mentioned that middle school sports specifically do not cover the cost 
of the sport, which I now have two seasons of middle school sports under my belt, with my oldest, I 
don’t know how that is possible.  Field hockey and track, they are using hand me down jerseys, field 
hockey had 11 games last year and one official per game at $50 per official.  Track had zero officials, 
the coaches were the officials and they are using hand me down jerseys from varsity.  I don’t know how 
track costs anything other than overhead allocations, which I would call programmatic cost.  The 
programmatic costs appear to be transportation and allocation such as the athletic director, insurance, 
hudl feels etc.  The stipends would actual be the operating costs not the programmatic costs.  Operating 
costs in my mind are direct costs associated with the sport.  Another clarification I would like to make is 
related to MIAA rules, 6th grades are not allowed to play on JV or Varsity sports, only 7th and 8th 
graders, if there is room, that would pretty much eliminate football, basketball, baseball, softball, any 
major sport, there probably is not enough room.  Track and field hockey are not offered at the middle 
school level at any clubs locally.  The first time picking up a field hockey stick for a lot of these girls is 
6th grade, last year the varsity field hockey team won the Midwach League undefeated, it is a very good 
program.  There were 20 girls on the turf this morning working out to try and repeat that for the 
upcoming season.  Middle school sports was $19,400 revenue positive, effectively off setting football.  
So we made the decision to cut middle school sports because of a funding issue and they are revenue 
positive, I don’t see why we are doing that.  Additionally, they are not under the MIAA, the coaches 
===PAGE 3===
 
1025 Massachusetts Avenue, Lunenburg, Massachusetts 01462

make the schedules, so it is really easy, you don’t have to contact anyone, the AD is not involved.  I 
know Coach Rios kind of scheduled the games last year.  We started the field hockey season with 
actually no games scheduled and finished the year with 12.  I have put in a formal request records to Dr. 
Fortuna and the school to help out, I would like the actuals for this.  These are what I would consider 
estimates moving forward and myself and some other parents are happy to provide this analysis for you.   
Chris Hurlbut 469 Sunny Hill Road Education is about more than academics alone.  Athletics are an 
integral part of the educational experience. Sports build teamwork, discipline, leadership, accountability, 
and school pride.  For many students, putting on a Lunenburg uniform is one of the most meaningful 
parts of their school experience.  Following the failed override, middle school athletics were eliminated 
as a budget reduction of $14,415 from roughly a $29 million budget.  Since that decision was made a 
growing number of parents and community members have come together with a shared goal, preserving 
these opportunities for our students.  Community members have already begun making financial 
commitments and are prepared to fund the gap if an approved path forward can be identified.  
Community members have asked straightforward questions regarding the actual cost of middle school 
athletics and the process required to apply community raised funds towards these programs.  Yet the 
answers have been inconsistent.  As a result, the community still lacks a clear understanding of both the 
financial picture and the path forward.  Based on information shared publicly, there are also legitimate 
questions regarding the actual financial impact of middle school athletics on the district.  Available data 
suggests that user registration fees may exceed the known cost of operating these programs, suggesting 
that middle school athletics may generate net revenue for the district.  If accurate, this would mean the 
district eliminated programs that were operating in the black.   We are not asking the school committee 
to solve this problem alone.  We are asking for accurate information and a clear path forward so that we 
can help solve it.  To move this effort forward in time for the upcoming athletic season, the community 
is requesting these three things by mid-July.  First a detailed breakdown of costs and revenue for each 
sport, including transportation, overhead, administrative expenses, and user fees.  Second, guidance on 
the process required for community raised funds or donations to support these programs. Third a 
commitment to work with the athletic directors, coaches, and schedulers to restore middle school 
athletics for the upcoming year.  This is a solvable problem if we work together.  Finally, I want to 
address the future of athletics and extracurricular activities in Lunenburg more broadly. The school 
committee has recently stated that looking ahead to next year and I quote “All extracurricular activities, 
including all athletics, will be on the table for future reductions, and that would result in a full 
dismantling of Lunenburg schools, if the district’s financial challenges persist”.  If the district leadership 
believes these programs may be at risk, the community deserves to know right now. 
Nikki Jeannotte, 111 Mulpus Road,  I am the president of the football boosters and I am here tonight 
on behalf of the boosters and the football program at the high school and also the head coach who just 
had a baby a couple days ago and wasn’t able to be here. Casey, the head coach put in a request to the 
athletic department to purchase five new helmets earlier this spring, we currently have more heads than 
we have helmets, we haven’t heard whether that was approved or denied.  If it is denied, which is 
probably given our financial predicament, we are hoping to get permission to purchase the helmets out 
of the booster funds, which we understand, we need permission from the school committee for.  The 
second matter is a bit trickier, I will preface with some background, we are all aware transportation to 
and from away games has been cut form the athletic budget.  The football boosters would like to fund 
one-way transportation to away games and have families bring their players and equipment home, which 
we have been told may or may not be a violation of Title IX, because there isn’t a female team with 
enough funds to purchase transportation.  However, it is our understanding that two mini buses were 
purchased by the district to help provide transportation to and from away games.  These buses are not a 
reasonable option for the football team given the number of athletes who travel and the amount of 
equipment they require.  With that in mind, any question of inequity stemming from football boosters 
funding alternative transportations should be settled because the mini buses aren’t a viable option for us 
===PAGE 4===
 
1025 Massachusetts Avenue, Lunenburg, Massachusetts 01462 
978 582-4100 ~ fax 978 582-4103 
and they are a viable option for many of the other smaller teams.  Our asks are first and ideally that the 
athletics department purchase helmets for the program, but if they can’t or are unable to, we have 
permission to purchase the helmets from boosters and also that we have permission to fund 
transportation to away games, which comes to about $1500.  I’d also like to add that I was on a zoom a 
couple weeks ago with several other booster people and you all stated that football is one of the 
wealthier programs, more wealthy than the other programs, and I respect that people believe this and I 
understand why, but we have more money because we work our tails off, fundraising at every available 
opportunity.  We do 50/50 at every game, we sell merch at every game, we latch on to any fundraising 
opportunity we can, we don’t get a dollar for admissions to the football games, we don’t get a dollar 
from concessions at football games, we don’t see any of that money.  We are basically asking to spend 
our money that we work really hard for in a responsible and meaningful way for our program. 
Mr. Sculimbrene I was unable to attend the boosters meeting, I want to make sure that as we go 
through these discussions that everybody keeps in mind that a solution that only solves one of the 
problems is not going to work.  If we solve a problem for one sport without considering the legal 
implications for other sports, all we are doing is inviting costs related to litigation, and those kinds of 
costs would be huge and they would be difficult to sustain and would not necessarily go away within a 
single calendar year.  So if sports and the booster organizations are trying to solve the problem, which it 
sounds like everybody wants to we need to make sure that it’s not just a numbers thing, that it is also a 
legal thing and that we work together to make sure that as much as we can we can comply with the law.  
One idea would be to pair sports, if you have a shared interest and shared facility, such as shared 
officiating boys’ and girls’ basketball that would be a natural pairing.  Some sports do not have 
necessarily natural pairings and some sports are automatically Title IX compliant, but it would be 
problematic and really sad if everybody puts all this effort in and then it all gets washed away because 
we didn’t comply with the law.  Solutions need to be mindful of those legal requirements, otherwise we 
just spend money that will be taken away by lawyers, which I don’t want to have happen. 
 
4. Chairs Report – lt is officially the end of the school year, so, thank you very much to Dr. Fortuna for 
her first successful Lunenburg school year and for getting us through this very difficult year. So, thank 
you. I very much appreciate it. For the foreseeable future, the school committee will be meeting here in 
the media room at the Middle High school. The town hall meeting room is sadly not handicap 
accessible. There's serious accessibility concerns with that meeting space and we can meet here. I 
understand this makes it more difficult for some people to watch the meetings remotely via public access 
and I am very sorry about that. You can still watch our meetings via Zoom when it works, and the 
recording of the meetings will be available the next day on the Lunenburg Public Access YouTube 
channel and Facebook. Just a plug, this town needs meeting spaces that are accessible to all members of 
the public, and all meetings should be taking place in handicap accessible places that don't require a 
terrifying outdated ancient lift to the second floor. I want to state up front that I will be asking the 
committee to vote to ask for a special town meeting as soon as legally possible. Once we have the final 
conference committee state budget. Also there was new snow and ice money that the town got.  It's 
looking very likely that the state legislature will be sending us extra chapter 70 money specifically 
address the statewide ongoing education funding crisis. This money as chapter 70 funding is intended 
for public education in the schools, 35% of the entire school budget in Luneburg is covered by chapter 
70 funds uh which are dispersed to the town but intended to go towards education. And I would like to 
ask that we have a special meeting to reopen the FY27 municipal budget once the state has finalized the 
number and that we allocate all of that additional chapter 70 money to the school budget. Things are 
going to be hard next year. That's not news to anyone. They're going to be very hard, they were already 
hard this year. This was not an easy school year because of the cuts that we had to make the year before. 
But this next upcoming school year, things are going to start breaking. I am really sorry. This affects my 
kid too.  Many things that you as a parent or you as a citizen or you as a student care about will be 
===PAGE 5===
 
1025 Massachusetts Avenue, Lunenburg, Massachusetts 01462 
978 582-4100 ~ fax 978 582-4103 
affected negatively. Many other things that may not affect you personally but affect other people in the 
town will be impacted negatively as well. I understand the urge to try and save specifically the things 
that matter to you. I really love the energy to try and make things better. I love the way our community 
continues to try. I love that we keep trying even in such adverse conditions and we don't give up. That's 
what it takes to change the world is a group of committed people working together to bring about the 
change they want to see in the world. But I want to make really clear as school committee members, it is 
our job to see the big picture to take in the entirety of the system and to look out for all members of the 
system. It is our job to keep an eye on the main goal and our core mission which is educating children 
and preparing students for the best possible future for each and every student. That is our job as school 
committee members. So I understand and I especially understand as a parent the impulse to fight 
precisely for what matters personally to you and your child. I don't ever want to stop anyone from 
advocating for your children and you should be very proud that you are good advocates for your 
children. But please just know that sometimes there are going to be important critical things that may not 
be as popular or have as many or any advocates, but are still vitally important. My example for that is 
toilet paper. No one is lining up to advocate that we buy it, but we really need it. It's something we have 
to spend money on. It's not visible. It's not something you think about. It is an expense, but there's 
nobody advocating for that, and it is our job as school committee members to look at the big picture and 
keep the big picture in mind. And I also want people to be cognizant of administration time and our staff 
time as we are facing such a dire gap between our needs and what we have. Requests are going to take 
longer. We're not going to have everything. Certain things you ask for may not exist or cannot be 
created because of the time. We are focusing on the core mission and we do not have enough personnel 
to get everything done. It is important that we together collectively as people who want to improve 
things who want to maintain quality schools in Luneburg and all that that means it's important we keep 
our eyes on the big picture and not turn into infighting over specific programs or issues. The big picture, 
adequate school funding for the entire system is where your efforts are going to do the most good. 
Continuing to engage the select boards, send emails, go to meetings, advocate for the school. So, run for 
office. Go to meetings twice because it's important. Make the phone calls. Email your state senator and 
state rep. Be a fierce advocate for every kid in this town and making sure that everyone is getting what 
they need.

8. Superintendent’s Report Dr. Fortuna shares updates on some key areas that have been focused on.  We 
were successful in our bid for transportation vans, we are currently waiting on the state contract and 
===PAGE 6===
 
1025 Massachusetts Avenue, Lunenburg, Massachusetts 01462 
978 582-4100 ~ fax 978 582-4103 
after receiving that we will be able to execute quickly to meet the spending deadline of June 30.  We 
will be able to purchase two new vans and have enough left over to cover driver training, registration, 
and insurance.  On the facilities front, we have secured an additional $175,000 in state earmarks for FY 
27 specifically for facilities improvements, one of the priorities will be making the main doors at the 
central office fully handicapped accessible, anticipated projects include ADA bathroom sinks at Turkey 
Hill, interior and exterior doors at primary and add a DMT antenna to remotely control security systems 
the JACE upgrade at LMHS, the generator install at Primary, a fan replacement in the hallway and gym 
at the Primary school, the installation of new gym lighting at LMHS and lift rental or purchase, this list 
is subject to change.  We are looking into possible portable AC in the upstairs at Turkey Hill also.  
Staffing and personnel updates include: Ms. Tousignant will be the new Assistant Principal of the 
Middle School, Mr. McGrath will take over as principal at Turkey Hill.  We have hired two new 
assistant principals, Kaitlyn Angulo at Turkey Hill and Eddaliz Viola at the High School. Dr. Fortuna 
lists a few positions that are currently open and are recruiting for, while also working closely with Dr. 
Mancini to develop a comprehensive plan for how athletics will operate and be supported when the 
season starts again.  The process with Title IX and boosters’ guidance is explained.

11. Public Comments for issues discussed tonight Laura Barooshian, 389 Mulpus Road I did find the 
fundraising policy that you mentioned, it has not been updated since 2019. And one thing that just caught 
my eye is it does say that families and athletes will not be asked to participate in more than two fundraisers 
per sport per year. So just as the groups are getting themselves organized.  I am here as president of the 
Music Gators  we had a meeting we talked about fundraising, that was the major topic of discussion, we do 
want to work with you guys to figure out what we can fundraise for, transportation is one of the items. Also 
part of our mission is to provide the schools with the uniforms.  I am very pleased we are going to be 
pushing 30 kids in marching band. That's up from 18. Ms McGregor is hopeful that we're going to be band 
and color guard. We are going to need to purchase color guard uniforms. We have different people of 
different sizes and different shapes and different needs this year. Um I'm still working on getting information 
from Ms. McGregor, I can't wait until band camp at the end of August to order color guard uniforms or we will 
not have them for football season. So we have a pool of funds available we are looking to donate, and we 
are looking for guidance on how to present/use them.  – email Liz Peterson, and it will be on the agenda for 
July 29th.  Also I can tell you the juniors are already feeling the stresses of having to choose certain classes 
and making choices of can I take an AP class, or honors class, or is it going to have to be a required 
elective.  If there is anything we can do to help restore the 6th grade Jazz band, please let us know.

SCHOOL COMMITTEE SPECIAL MEETING 
Date: Wednesday, July 15, 2026
